I’m not sure… I mean, I think there are moments, like when music comes on and I catch myself moving before I’ve decided to. That still feels like something, maybe not exactly pleasure, but familiar.

And sometimes I like arranging things—like notebooks, scraps from rehearsals, old programs. Though then I get caught reading the names and remembering who stood where in a piece from years ago, and suddenly I’m not really enjoying it, I’m just… somewhere else in my head.

So I guess I don’t know if I can name a clear thing. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.

community down, even though people have been kinder about it than I deserve.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: Even with the injury, I couldn’t stop moving from task to task—drawing maps, emailing collaborators, reorganizing music files, and trying to redesign the whole project in one rush.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I honestly believed I could turn this setback into a career-defining work that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, and I was shocked when others didn’t immediately see it.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My collaborators told me I was talking so fast in meetings that they couldn’t get a word in, and I remember feeling like if I paused the whole idea would presenters, dancers I barely knew, and old professors with long proposals, as if I had to build an entire new network for the piece right that minute.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: the wired stretch when I was barely sleeping and racing through plans. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a neat way. I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.

Yeah… my energy is strange. It’s not like normal tired after a rehearsal, where at least it makes sense because you’ve used your body. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, and not even a complicated one—and I feel completely wiped out.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because lately I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels. I’ll wake up and just feel… empty, before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The heavy part, the hollow sadness. I remember because it sort of started taking over around the time I was working on this one piece, and I kept blaming the choreography or the music or the studio being too cold—but no, it’s been there most mornings for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in dancing or has that changed for you over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Dancing is… it’s complicated, you know? Sometimes I’m in the studio and my body still knows what to do, like muscle memory just takes over, and then I’ll get distracted thinking about the scuff marks on the floor or how one of my old teachers used to say you can tell someone’s whole life by how they enter a room.\n\nSo… pleasure? I don’t know. It’s changed, I think, but I can’t quite pin down how.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things you used to enjoy that don't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I used to like little things, like going to cafés after rehearsal or looking through old costume sketches, but then I start thinking about the fabric, and how my friend once found this incredible blue silk in a thrift shop, and we were going to make something with it but never did.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if those things don’t bring me pleasure anymore, or if I just don’t stay with them long enough to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a neat way. I think things feel different, but I can’t tell you exactly what still feels enjoyable and what doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think there are moments, like when music comes on and I catch myself moving before I’ve decided to. That still feels like something, maybe not exactly pleasure, but familiar.\n\nAnd sometimes I like arranging things—like notebooks, scraps from rehearsals, old programs. Though then I get caught reading the names and remembering who stood where in a piece from years ago, and suddenly I’m not really enjoying it, I’m just… somewhere else in my head.\n\nSo I guess I don’t know if I can name a clear thing. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep going back over the performances that got cancelled, like I’m standing in the theater after everyone’s left and I can still see where people were supposed to be.\n\nI tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community in a way. People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. I keep expecting to get back up and feel reset, and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… but I feel odd saying it that way because it wasn’t like a person standing in the room talking to me.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I heard this low humming voice—almost like wind moving over rock, if that makes sense. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.\n\nWishing I were dead… I’m not sure. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. I keep expecting to get back up and feel reset, and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… but I feel odd saying it that way because it wasn’t like a person standing in the room talking to me.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I heard this low humming voice—almost like wind moving over rock, if that makes sense. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.

For a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Like not just inspired by them, but instructed by them. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. Even strangers saying ordinary things would feel like instructions, like they were unknowingly passing me messages.

And I know trail signs are just trail signs. I do know that now. But at the time it felt very direct. Like it was all arranged around the work.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because lately I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels. I’ll wake up and just feel… empty, before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The heavy part, the hollow sadness. I remember because it sort of started taking over around the time I was working on this one piece, and I kept blaming the choreography or the music or the studio being too cold—but no, it’s been there most mornings for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in dancing or has that changed for you over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Dancing is… it’s complicated, you know? Sometimes I’m in the studio and my body still knows what to do, like muscle memory just takes over, and then I’ll get distracted thinking about the scuff marks on the floor or how one of my old teachers used to say you can tell someone’s whole life by how they enter a room.\n\nSo… pleasure? I don’t know. It’s changed, I think, but I can’t quite pin down how.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things you used to enjoy that don't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I used to like little things, like going to cafés after rehearsal or looking through old costume sketches, but then I start thinking about the fabric, and how my friend once found this incredible blue silk in a thrift shop, and we were going to make something with it but never did.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if those things don’t bring me pleasure anymore, or if I just don’t stay with them long enough to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a neat way. I think things feel different, but I can’t tell you exactly what still feels enjoyable and what doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think there are moments, like when music comes on and I catch myself moving before I’ve decided to. That still feels like something, maybe not exactly pleasure, but familiar.\n\nAnd sometimes I like arranging things—like notebooks, scraps from rehearsals, old programs. Though then I get caught reading the names and remembering who stood where in a piece from years ago, and suddenly I’m not really enjoying it, I’m just… somewhere else in my head.\n\nSo I guess I don’t know if I can name a clear thing. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep going back over the performances that got cancelled, like I’m standing in the theater after everyone’s left and I can still see where people were supposed to be.\n\nI tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community in a way. People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More slow, I think. Like… recently even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like this huge effort, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.

Which is frustrating, because usually in a studio I know exactly where my limbs are going, even if I’m tired. I’ll see the notebook sitting there and think, just go get it, it’s ten steps, and then somehow it feels like I’m dragging myself across the floor. Not dramatic, just… slowed down.

Dancing is… it’s complicated, you know? Sometimes I’m in the studio and my body still knows what to do, like muscle memory just takes over, and then I’ll get distracted thinking about the scuff marks on the floor or how one of my old teachers used to say you can tell someone’s whole life by how they enter a room.\n\nSo… pleasure? I don’t know. It’s changed, I think, but I can’t quite pin down how.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things you used to enjoy that don't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. The heavy part, the hollow sadness. I remember because it sort of started taking over around the time I was working on this one piece, and I kept blaming the choreography or the music or the studio being too cold—but no, it’s been there most mornings for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in dancing or has that changed for you over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Dancing is… it’s complicated, you know? Sometimes I’m in the studio and my body still knows what to do, like muscle memory just takes over, and then I’ll get distracted thinking about the scuff marks on the floor or how one of my old teachers used to say you can tell someone’s whole life by how they enter a room.\n\nSo… pleasure? I don’t know. It’s changed, I think, but I can’t quite pin down how.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things you used to enjoy that don't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I used to like little things, like going to cafés after rehearsal or looking through old costume sketches, but then I start thinking about the fabric, and how my friend once found this incredible blue silk in a thrift shop, and we were going to make something with it but never did.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if those things don’t bring me pleasure anymore, or if I just don’t stay with them long enough to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a neat way. I think things feel different, but I can’t tell you exactly what still feels enjoyable and what doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think there are moments, like when music comes on and I catch myself moving before I’ve decided to. the wired stretch when I was barely sleeping and racing through plans. Since my mood dropped, I still feel guilty and slowed down, but I haven’t heard the voice or believed the signs were directing me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I fell behind on teaching prep and missed a grant deadline, and my studio got so chaotic that collaborators started handling basic scheduling for me. I’m still showing up for essentials, but I’m not functioning like I normally do as a choreographer or teacher.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole pattern started after my ankle injury and has been going on for about two months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The depressed part has been present for about nine months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Like not just inspired by them, but instructed by them. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. Even strangers saying ordinary things would feel like instructions, like they were unknowingly passing me messages.\n\nAnd I know trail signs are just trail signs. I do know that now. But at the time it felt very direct. Like it was all arranged around the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up and can't calm down?\n\nPatient: More slow, I think. Like… recently even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like this huge effort, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because usually in a studio I know exactly where my limbs are going, even if I’m tired. I’ll see the notebook sitting there and think, just go get it, it’s ten steps, and then somehow it feels like I’m dragging myself across the floor. Not dramatic, just… slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but my rehearsal clothes are definitely looser—like the waistbands and the leotards don’t sit the same.\n\nWhen I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch and dinner because I was working through phrases. And now it’s different… food just tastes kind of flat, like cardboard, so I don’t want much of it. It’s annoying, because dancers are always noticing fabric and fit anyway, but yes, I think I’ve lost weight.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever seen things that other people couldn't see?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Seen things? Not really like… figures in the room or anything. It was more hearing.

On a few of the really wired days, I would hear this low humming voice, almost like wind moving over rock, and it would tell me where the dancers should go. Which sounds theatrical, I know—dancers are always talking about “listening to the space,” and one of my old teachers used to make us improvise with our eyes closed for ages—but this was different.

I’d be alone in my apartment, windows closed, and still hear it, like it was giving directions for the movement. So… not seeing, exactly. More that.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because lately I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels. I’ll wake up and just feel… empty, before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The heavy part, the hollow sadness. I remember because it sort of started taking over around the time I was working on this one piece, and I kept blaming the choreography or the music or the studio being too cold—but no, it’s been there most mornings for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in dancing or has that changed for you over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Dancing is… it’s complicated, you know? Sometimes I’m in the studio and my body still knows what to do, like muscle memory just takes over, and then I’ll get distracted thinking about the scuff marks on the floor or how one of my old teachers used to say you can tell someone’s whole life by how they enter a room.\n\nSo… pleasure? I don’t know. It’s changed, I think, but I can’t quite pin down how.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things you used to enjoy that don't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I used to like little things, like going to cafés after rehearsal or looking through old costume sketches, but then I start thinking about the fabric, and how my friend once found this incredible blue silk in a thrift shop, and we were going to make something with it but never did.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if those things don’t bring me pleasure anymore, or if I just don’t stay with them long enough to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a neat way. I think things feel different, but I can’t tell you exactly what still feels enjoyable and what doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think there are moments, like when music comes on and I catch myself moving before I’ve decided to. That still feels like something, maybe not exactly pleasure, but familiar.\n\nAnd sometimes I like arranging things—like notebooks, scraps from rehearsals, old programs. Though then I get caught reading the names and remembering who stood where in a piece from years ago, and suddenly I’m not really enjoying it, I’m just… somewhere else in my head.\n\nSo I guess I don’t know if I can name a clear thing. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep going back over the performances that got cancelled, like I’m standing in the theater after everyone’s left and I can still see where people were supposed to be.\n\nI tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community in a way. People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.\n\nWishing I were dead… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought of it in a direct way like that. Sometimes I just want everything to stop being so heavy, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. It gets tangled up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels or how quickly you can get things done?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my energy is strange. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment. And I remember checking the windows because I thought maybe it was outside, but they were closed.\n\nIt sounds strange when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Special powers, no… not like flying or reading minds. But I did feel like I had this rare… almost singular ability to make something out of the mess.

After the setback, I honestly thought, okay, this is the thing—this is going to become the work that defines me. Not just a good piece, but something that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, like those two things had been waiting for me to connect them properly.

And I remember being genuinely shocked when other people didn’t immediately understand that. Like, why aren’t you seeing it? It felt so obvious to me at the time.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because lately I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels. I’ll wake up and just feel… empty, before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The heavy part, the hollow sadness. I remember because it sort of started taking over around the time I was working on this one piece, and I kept blaming the choreography or the music or the studio being too cold—but no, it’s been there most mornings for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in dancing or has that changed for you over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Dancing is… it’s complicated, you know? Sometimes I’m in the studio and my body still knows what to do, like muscle memory just takes over, and then I’ll get distracted thinking about the scuff marks on the floor or how one of my old teachers used to say you can tell someone’s whole life by how they enter a room.\n\nSo… pleasure? I don’t know. It’s changed, I think, but I can’t quite pin down how.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things you used to enjoy that don't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I used to like little things, like going to cafés after rehearsal or looking through old costume sketches, but then I start thinking about the fabric, and how my friend once found this incredible blue silk in a thrift shop, and we were going to make something with it but never did.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if those things don’t bring me pleasure anymore, or if I just don’t stay with them long enough to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a neat way. I think things feel different, but I can’t tell you exactly what still feels enjoyable and what doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think there are moments, like when music comes on and I catch myself moving before I’ve decided to. That still feels like something, maybe not exactly pleasure, but familiar.\n\nAnd sometimes I like arranging things—like notebooks, scraps from rehearsals, old programs. Though then I get caught reading the names and remembering who stood where in a piece from years ago, and suddenly I’m not really enjoying it, I’m just… somewhere else in my head.\n\nSo I guess I don’t know if I can name a clear thing. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep going back over the performances that got cancelled, like I’m standing in the theater after everyone’s left and I can still see where people were supposed to be.\n\nI tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community in a way. People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.\n\nWishing I were dead… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought of it in a direct way like that. Sometimes I just want everything to stop being so heavy, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. It gets tangled up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels or how quickly you can get things done?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my energy is strange. It’s not like normal tired after a rehearsal, where at least it makes sense because you’ve used your body. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, and not even a complicated one—and I feel completely wiped out.\n\nEven showering is this whole production now, especially with the ankle brace, trying not to slip, balancing awkwardly, and then afterward I just want to lie down. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. I keep expecting to get back up and feel reset, and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… but I feel odd saying it that way because it wasn’t like a person standing in the room talking to me.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I heard this low humming voice—almost like wind moving over rock, if that makes sense. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment. And I remember checking the windows because I thought maybe it was outside, but they were closed.\n\nIt sounds strange when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Like not just inspired by them, but instructed by them. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. Even strangers saying ordinary things would feel like instructions, like they were unknowingly passing me messages.\n\nAnd I know trail signs are just trail signs. I do know that now. But at the time it felt very direct. Like it was all arranged around the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up and can't calm down?\n\nPatient: More slow, I think. Like… recently even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like this huge effort, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because usually in a studio I know exactly where my limbs are going, even if I’m tired. I’ll see the notebook sitting there and think, just go get it, it’s ten steps, and then somehow it feels like I’m dragging myself across the floor. Not dramatic, just… slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but my rehearsal clothes are definitely looser—like the waistbands and the leotards don’t sit the same.\n\nWhen I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch and dinner because I was working through phrases. And now it’s different… food just tastes kind of flat, like cardboard, so I don’t want much of it. It’s annoying, because dancers are always noticing fabric and fit anyway, but yes, I think I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not really like… figures in the room or anything. It was more hearing.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I would hear this low humming voice, almost like wind moving over rock, and it would tell me where the dancers should go. Which sounds theatrical, I know—dancers are always talking about “listening to the space,” and one of my old teachers used to make us improvise with our eyes closed for ages—but this was different.\n\nI’d be alone in my apartment, windows closed, and still hear it, like it was giving directions for the movement. So… not seeing, exactly. More that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that you had special powers or abilities that no one else has?\n\nPatient: Special powers, no… not like flying or reading minds. But I did feel like I had this rare… almost singular ability to make something out of the mess.\n\nAfter the setback, I honestly thought, okay, this is the thing—this is going to become the work that defines me. Not just a good piece, but something that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, like those two things had been waiting for me to connect them properly.\n\nAnd I remember being genuinely shocked when other people didn’t immediately understand that. Like, why aren’t you seeing it? It felt so obvious to me at the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I keep expecting to get back up and feel reset, and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… but I feel odd saying it that way because it wasn’t like a person standing in the room talking to me.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I heard this low humming voice—almost like wind moving over rock, if that makes sense. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment. And I remember checking the windows because I thought maybe it was outside, but they were closed.\n\nIt sounds strange when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Like not just inspired by them, but instructed by them. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. Even strangers saying ordinary things would feel like instructions, like they were unknowingly passing me messages.\n\nAnd I know trail signs are just trail signs. I do know that now. But at the time it felt very direct. Like it was all arranged around the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up and can't calm down?\n\nPatient: More slow, I think. Like… recently even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like this huge effort, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because usually in a studio I know exactly where my limbs are going, even if I’m tired. I’ll see the notebook sitting there and think, just go get it, it’s ten steps, and then somehow it feels like I’m dragging myself across the floor. Not dramatic, just… slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but my rehearsal clothes are definitely looser—like the waistbands and the leotards don’t sit the same.\n\nWhen I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch and dinner because I was working through phrases. And now it’s different… food just tastes kind of flat, like cardboard, so I don’t want much of it. It’s annoying, because dancers are always noticing fabric and fit anyway, but yes, I think I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not really like… figures in the room or anything. It was more hearing.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I would hear this low humming voice, almost like wind moving over rock, and it would tell me where the dancers should go. Which sounds theatrical, I know—dancers are always talking about “listening to the space,” and one of my old teachers used to make us improvise with our eyes closed for ages—but this was different.\n\nI’d be alone in my apartment, windows closed, and still hear it, like it was giving directions for the movement. So… not seeing, exactly. More that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that you had special powers or abilities that no one else has?\n\nPatient: Special powers, no… not like flying or reading minds. But I did feel like I had this rare… almost singular ability to make something out of the mess.\n\nAfter the setback, I honestly thought, okay, this is the thing—this is going to become the work that defines me. Not just a good piece, but something that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, like those two things had been waiting for me to connect them properly.\n\nAnd I remember being genuinely shocked when other people didn’t immediately understand that. Like, why aren’t you seeing it? It felt so obvious to me at the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Kind of… not like someone had a remote control for my thoughts, exactly. But for a while I was completely sure the mountains had picked me, singled me out, to make this piece that was going to matter historically.

People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.\n\nWishing I were dead… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought of it in a direct way like that. Sometimes I just want everything to stop being so heavy, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment. And I remember checking the windows because I thought maybe it was outside, but they were closed.\n\nIt sounds strange when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. But at the time it felt very direct. Like it was all arranged around the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up and can't calm down?\n\nPatient: More slow, I think. Like… recently even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like this huge effort, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because usually in a studio I know exactly where my limbs are going, even if I’m tired. It’s annoying, because dancers are always noticing fabric and fit anyway, but yes, I think I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not really like… figures in the room or anything. It was more hearing.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I would hear this low humming voice, almost like wind moving over rock, and it would tell me where the dancers should go. Which sounds theatrical, I know—dancers are always talking about “listening to the space,” and one of my old teachers used to make us improvise with our eyes closed for ages—but this was different.\n\nI’d be alone in my apartment, windows closed, and still hear it, like it was giving directions for the movement. So… not seeing, exactly. More that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that you had special powers or abilities that no one else has?\n\nPatient: Special powers, no… not like flying or reading minds. But I did feel like I had this rare… almost singular ability to make something out of the mess.\n\nAfter the setback, I honestly thought, okay, this is the thing—this is going to become the work that defines me. Not just a good piece, but something that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, like those two things had been waiting for me to connect them properly.\n\nAnd I remember being genuinely shocked when other people didn’t immediately understand that. Like, why aren’t you seeing it? It felt so obvious to me at the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were being controlled or influenced by something outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: Kind of… not like someone had a remote control for my thoughts, exactly. But for a while I was completely sure the mountains had picked me, singled me out, to make this piece that was going to matter historically.\n\nAnd then everything started feeling like it was aimed at me—trail signs, weather reports, even random things strangers said, like if someone in line mentioned “a storm coming,” I’d think, oh, that’s an instruction, that’s about the structure of the work. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.\n\nWishing I were dead… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought of it in a direct way like that. Sometimes I just want everything to stop being so heavy, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. I keep expecting to get back up and feel reset, and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… but I feel odd saying it that way because it wasn’t like a person standing in the room talking to me.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I heard this low humming voice—almost like wind moving over rock, if that makes sense. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment. And I remember checking the windows because I thought maybe it was outside, but they were closed.\n\nIt sounds strange when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Like not just inspired by them, but instructed by them. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. But at the time it felt very direct. And now it’s different… food just tastes kind of flat, like cardboard, so I don’t want much of it. It’s annoying, because dancers are always noticing fabric and fit anyway, but yes, I think I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not really like… figures in the room or anything. Which sounds theatrical, I know—dancers are always talking about “listening to the space,” and one of my old teachers used to make us improvise with our eyes closed for ages—but this was different.\n\nI’d be alone in my apartment, windows closed, and still hear it, like it was giving directions for the movement. That still feels like something, maybe not exactly pleasure, but familiar.\n\nAnd sometimes I like arranging things—like notebooks, scraps from rehearsals, old programs. Though then I get caught reading the names and remembering who stood where in a piece from years ago, and suddenly I’m not really enjoying it, I’m just… somewhere else in my head.\n\nSo I guess I don’t know if I can name a clear thing. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep going back over the performances that got cancelled, like I’m standing in the theater after everyone’s left and I can still see where people were supposed to be.\n\nI tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community in a way. People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.\n\nWishing I were dead… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought of it in a direct way like that. Sometimes I just want everything to stop being so heavy, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. It gets tangled up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels or how quickly you can get things done?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my energy is strange. It’s not like normal tired after a rehearsal, where at least it makes sense because you’ve used your body. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, and not even a complicated one—and I feel completely wiped out.\n\nEven showering is this whole production now, especially with the ankle brace, trying not to slip, balancing awkwardly, and then afterward I just want to lie down. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. I keep expecting to get back up and feel reset, and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… but I feel odd saying it that way because it wasn’t like a person standing in the room talking to me.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I heard this low humming voice—almost like wind moving over rock, if that makes sense. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment. And I remember checking the windows because I thought maybe it was outside, but they were closed.\n\nIt sounds strange when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Like not just inspired by them, but instructed by them. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. Even strangers saying ordinary things would feel like instructions, like they were unknowingly passing me messages.\n\nAnd I know trail signs are just trail signs. I do know that now. But at the time it felt very direct. Like it was all arranged around the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up and can't calm down?\n\nPatient: More slow, I think. Like… recently even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like this huge effort, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because usually in a studio I know exactly where my limbs are going, even if I’m tired. I’ll see the notebook sitting there and think, just go get it, it’s ten steps, and then somehow it feels like I’m dragging myself across the floor. Not dramatic, just… slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but my rehearsal clothes are definitely looser—like the waistbands and the leotards don’t sit the same.\n\nWhen I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch and dinner because I was working through phrases. And now it’s different… food just tastes kind of flat, like cardboard, so I don’t want much of it. It’s annoying, because dancers are always noticing fabric and fit anyway, but yes, I think I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not really like… figures in the room or anything. It was more hearing.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I would hear this low humming voice, almost like wind moving over rock, and it would tell me where the dancers should go. Which sounds theatrical, I know—dancers are always talking about “listening to the space,” and one of my old teachers used to make us improvise with our eyes closed for ages—but this was different.\n\nI’d be alone in my apartment, windows closed, and still hear it, like it was giving directions for the movement. So… not seeing, exactly. More that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that you had special powers or abilities that no one else has?\n\nPatient: Special powers, no… not like flying or reading minds. But I did feel like I had this rare… almost singular ability to make something out of the mess.\n\nAfter the setback, I honestly thought, okay, this is the thing—this is going to become the work that defines me. Not just a good piece, but something that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, like those two things had been waiting for me to connect them properly.\n\nAnd I remember being genuinely shocked when other people didn’t immediately understand that. Like, why aren’t you seeing it? It felt so obvious to me at the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were being controlled or influenced by something outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: Kind of… not like someone had a remote control for my thoughts, exactly. But for a while I was completely sure the mountains had picked me, singled me out, to make this piece that was going to matter historically.\n\nAnd then everything started feeling like it was aimed at me—trail signs, weather reports, even random things strangers said, like if someone in line mentioned “a storm coming,” I’d think, oh, that’s an instruction, that’s about the structure of the work. Which sounds… I know how it sounds. But at the time it felt very clear, like the outside world was guiding what I was supposed to make.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been consistently present over the past nine months or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s… come and gone in intensity, I guess, but the depressed part has pretty much been there for about nine months.\n\nNot every single hour looking identical—some days I could still get to rehearsal or answer emails, and then other days I’d just sort of sit there staring at the floor, thinking about how dancers always look so decisive from the outside, even when you’re completely hollow inside. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, yes—the low part has been around about nine months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It felt so obvious to me at the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were being controlled or influenced by something outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: Kind of… not like someone had a remote control for my thoughts, exactly. But for a while I was completely sure the mountains had picked me, singled me out, to make this piece that was going to matter historically.\n\nAnd then everything started feeling like it was aimed at me—trail signs, weather reports, even random things strangers said, like if someone in line mentioned “a storm coming,” I’d think, oh, that’s an instruction, that’s about the structure of the work. Which sounds… I know how it sounds. But at the time it felt very clear, like the outside world was guiding what I was supposed to make.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been consistently present over the past nine months or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s… come and gone in intensity, I guess, but the depressed part has pretty much been there for about nine months.\n\nNot every single hour looking identical—some days I could still get to rehearsal or answer emails, and then other days I’d just sort of sit there staring at the floor, thinking about how dancers always look so decisive from the outside, even when you’re completely hollow inside. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo, yes—the low part has been around about nine months.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you recently experienced any periods where you felt completely disconnected from reality?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"For a while I was completely sure the mountains had singled me out to make this historic piece, and I kept taking things like trail signs, weather reports, and even strangers’ comments as instructions meant only for me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say completely, because I could still buy coffee and talk to people and rehearse, but there was a stretch where reality felt like it had this extra layer on it.

Like with the mountains—I was sure they had singled me out, chosen me somehow, to make this historic piece. And then I’d see a trail sign or hear a weather report, or even some stranger saying something ordinary, and I’d take it as an instruction meant just for me. Like it was all connected to what I was supposed to create.

It’s strange saying it now. At the time it didn’t feel strange at all.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because lately I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels. I’ll wake up and just feel… empty, before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The heavy part, the hollow sadness. I remember because it sort of started taking over around the time I was working on this one piece, and I kept blaming the choreography or the music or the studio being too cold—but no, it’s been there most mornings for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in dancing or has that changed for you over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Dancing is… it’s complicated, you know? Sometimes I’m in the studio and my body still knows what to do, like muscle memory just takes over, and then I’ll get distracted thinking about the scuff marks on the floor or how one of my old teachers used to say you can tell someone’s whole life by how they enter a room.\n\nSo… pleasure? I don’t know. It’s changed, I think, but I can’t quite pin down how.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things you used to enjoy that don't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I used to like little things, like going to cafés after rehearsal or looking through old costume sketches, but then I start thinking about the fabric, and how my friend once found this incredible blue silk in a thrift shop, and we were going to make something with it but never did.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if those things don’t bring me pleasure anymore, or if I just don’t stay with them long enough to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a neat way. I think things feel different, but I can’t tell you exactly what still feels enjoyable and what doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think there are moments, like when music comes on and I catch myself moving before I’ve decided to. That still feels like something, maybe not exactly pleasure, but familiar.\n\nAnd sometimes I like arranging things—like notebooks, scraps from rehearsals, old programs. Though then I get caught reading the names and remembering who stood where in a piece from years ago, and suddenly I’m not really enjoying it, I’m just… somewhere else in my head.\n\nSo I guess I don’t know if I can name a clear thing. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep going back over the performances that got cancelled, like I’m standing in the theater after everyone’s left and I can still see where people were supposed to be.\n\nI tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community in a way. People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.\n\nWishing I were dead… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought of it in a direct way like that. Sometimes I just want everything to stop being so heavy, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. It gets tangled up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels or how quickly you can get things done?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my energy is strange. It’s not like normal tired after a rehearsal, where at least it makes sense because you’ve used your body. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, and not even a complicated one—and I feel completely wiped out.\n\nEven showering is this whole production now, especially with the ankle brace, trying not to slip, balancing awkwardly, and then afterward I just want to lie down. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. I keep expecting to get back up and feel reset, and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… but I feel odd saying it that way because it wasn’t like a person standing in the room talking to me.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I heard this low humming voice—almost like wind moving over rock, if that makes sense. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment. And I remember checking the windows because I thought maybe it was outside, but they were closed.\n\nIt sounds strange when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Like not just inspired by them, but instructed by them. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. Even strangers saying ordinary things would feel like instructions, like they were unknowingly passing me messages.\n\nAnd I know trail signs are just trail signs. I do know that now. But at the time it felt very direct. Like it was all arranged around the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up and can't calm down?\n\nPatient: More slow, I think. Like… recently even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like this huge effort, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because usually in a studio I know exactly where my limbs are going, even if I’m tired. I’ll see the notebook sitting there and think, just go get it, it’s ten steps, and then somehow it feels like I’m dragging myself across the floor. Not dramatic, just… slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but my rehearsal clothes are definitely looser—like the waistbands and the leotards don’t sit the same.\n\nWhen I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch and dinner because I was working through phrases. And now it’s different… food just tastes kind of flat, like cardboard, so I don’t want much of it. It’s annoying, because dancers are always noticing fabric and fit anyway, but yes, I think I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not really like… figures in the room or anything. It was more hearing.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I would hear this low humming voice, almost like wind moving over rock, and it would tell me where the dancers should go. Which sounds theatrical, I know—dancers are always talking about “listening to the space,” and one of my old teachers used to make us improvise with our eyes closed for ages—but this was different.\n\nI’d be alone in my apartment, windows closed, and still hear it, like it was giving directions for the movement. So… not seeing, exactly. More that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that you had special powers or abilities that no one else has?\n\nPatient: Special powers, no… not like flying or reading minds. Not just a good piece, but something that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, like those two things had been waiting for me to connect them properly.\n\nAnd I remember being genuinely shocked when other people didn’t immediately understand that. Like, why aren’t you seeing it? It felt so obvious to me at the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were being controlled or influenced by something outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: Kind of… not like someone had a remote control for my thoughts, exactly. But for a while I was completely sure the mountains had picked me, singled me out, to make this piece that was going to matter historically.\n\nAnd then everything started feeling like it was aimed at me—trail signs, weather reports, even random things strangers said, like if someone in line mentioned “a storm coming,” I’d think, oh, that’s an instruction, that’s about the structure of the work. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because lately I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels. I’ll wake up and just feel… empty, before the day even starts.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The heavy part, the hollow sadness. I remember because it sort of started taking over around the time I was working on this one piece, and I kept blaming the choreography or the music or the studio being too cold—but no, it’s been there most mornings for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in dancing or has that changed for you over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. Dancing is… it’s complicated, you know? Sometimes I’m in the studio and my body still knows what to do, like muscle memory just takes over, and then I’ll get distracted thinking about the scuff marks on the floor or how one of my old teachers used to say you can tell someone’s whole life by how they enter a room.\n\nSo… pleasure? I don’t know. It’s changed, I think, but I can’t quite pin down how.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things you used to enjoy that don't bring you pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but no longer find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of something clear, like “yes, this one thing,” but my mind keeps sliding off it. I mean, I used to like going out after rehearsal, little cafés, watching people walk by—dancers are terrible about watching how people walk, by the way, it’s like we can’t turn it off.\n\nBut whether I don’t enjoy it now, or I just don’t really… settle into it, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a neat way. I think things feel different, but I can’t tell you exactly what still feels enjoyable and what doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think there are moments, like when music comes on and I catch myself moving before I’ve decided to. That still feels like something, maybe not exactly pleasure, but familiar.\n\nAnd sometimes I like arranging things—like notebooks, scraps from rehearsals, old programs. Though then I get caught reading the names and remembering who stood where in a piece from years ago, and suddenly I’m not really enjoying it, I’m just… somewhere else in my head.\n\nSo I guess I don’t know if I can name a clear thing. There are pieces of enjoyment, maybe, but they don’t stay very neatly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep going back over the performances that got cancelled, like I’m standing in the theater after everyone’s left and I can still see where people were supposed to be.\n\nI tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community in a way. People have been kind about it—too kind, probably—and that almost makes it worse, because I keep thinking they’re being nicer than I deserve.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, when you say “harm,” I start thinking about all the ways dancers get told to ignore pain, and then that turns into this whole thing about discipline and punishment, which probably isn’t what you mean.\n\nWishing I were dead… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and thought of it in a direct way like that. Sometimes I just want everything to stop being so heavy, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. It gets tangled up in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels or how quickly you can get things done?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my energy is strange. It’s not like normal tired after a rehearsal, where at least it makes sense because you’ve used your body. But lying down doesn’t really refill anything. I keep expecting to get back up and feel reset, and I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… but I feel odd saying it that way because it wasn’t like a person standing in the room talking to me.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I heard this low humming voice—almost like wind moving over rock, if that makes sense. It would sort of tell me where the dancers should move, like placement or direction, even when I was alone in my apartment. And I remember checking the windows because I thought maybe it was outside, but they were closed.\n\nIt sounds strange when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other strange thoughts or perceptions besides the humming voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was another thing, though I keep wanting to explain it in terms of choreography because otherwise it sounds ridiculous.\n\nFor a while I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Like not just inspired by them, but instructed by them. I’d see a trail sign and feel like it was meant specifically for me, or hear a weather report and think, okay, that’s the next section, that’s the timing. Even strangers saying ordinary things would feel like instructions, like they were unknowingly passing me messages.\n\nAnd I know trail signs are just trail signs. I do know that now. But at the time it felt very direct. Like it was all arranged around the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up and can't calm down?\n\nPatient: More slow, I think. Like… recently even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like this huge effort, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n\nWhich is frustrating, because usually in a studio I know exactly where my limbs are going, even if I’m tired. I’ll see the notebook sitting there and think, just go get it, it’s ten steps, and then somehow it feels like I’m dragging myself across the floor. Not dramatic, just… slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but my rehearsal clothes are definitely looser—like the waistbands and the leotards don’t sit the same.\n\nWhen I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch and dinner because I was working through phrases. And now it’s different… food just tastes kind of flat, like cardboard, so I don’t want much of it. It’s annoying, because dancers are always noticing fabric and fit anyway, but yes, I think I’ve lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Seen things? Not really like… figures in the room or anything. It was more hearing.\n\nOn a few of the really wired days, I would hear this low humming voice, almost like wind moving over rock, and it would tell me where the dancers should go. Which sounds theatrical, I know—dancers are always talking about “listening to the space,” and one of my old teachers used to make us improvise with our eyes closed for ages—but this was different.\n\nI’d be alone in my apartment, windows closed, and still hear it, like it was giving directions for the movement. So… not seeing, exactly. More that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that you had special powers or abilities that no one else has?\n\nPatient: Special powers, no… not like flying or reading minds.
